Honestly, what finally pushed me to come in is how physically tense I’ve been. I mean, my shoulders and neck are just… clenched all day. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very much so. It’s not just one worry that I can point to and say, “That’s the problem.” It kind of spreads everywhere, and my mind just keeps circling things over and over.

Like, I’ll start thinking, what if I never get hired again if something goes wrong, or what if parents already think I’m not good at what I do? Then it becomes, have I ruined my reputation with families somehow? And then it jumps to bigger things, like whether retirement is slipping further away and I’m not going to be okay later on.

And then it can get really… I don’t know, irrational-feeling but still convincing in the moment. I’ll think about some tiny mistake during a story-time activity — like maybe I handled something awkwardly or didn’t explain something clearly — and suddenly my brain is saying, “Does that mean you shouldn’t be trusted with children?” Which is awful to even say out loud, because I care so much about the kids. But that’s where my mind goes. It just keeps finding another angle to worry from.

It didn’t just pass after a few weeks the way I hoped it would.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 53 / preschool_or_kindergarten_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Uncontrollable_Worry\nmanifestation: \"I try telling myself to make a list, pray, weed the garden, or focus on the next practical step, but one worry just hands off to another until I've lost half the afternoon to it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s probably one of the worst parts of it, honestly. I can sometimes catch it starting, and I’ll try to be very practical about it — like, “Okay, make a list. What actually needs to be done today?” Or I’ll pray, or I’ll go outside and weed the garden because that usually gives my hands something simple to do. Or I’ll tell myself, “Just focus on the next step, not the whole future, just the next thing.”

But it doesn’t really stop. It just… changes shape. One worry hands off to the next one, like a relay race I didn’t agree to be in. I’ll start with one concern, and then by the time I look up, I’m three or four worries away from where I started, and half the afternoon is gone. And I don’t even always know how I got there. I’ll think I’m problem-solving, but really I’m just circling and circling.

So yes, it’s very hard to control. I can distract myself for a few minutes sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding when it stops.

Or I’ll tell myself, “Just focus on the next step, not the whole future, just the next thing.”\n\nBut it doesn’t really stop. It just… changes shape. One worry hands off to the next one, like a relay race I didn’t agree to be in. I’ll start with one concern, and then by the time I look up, I’m three or four worries away from where I started, and half the afternoon is gone. And I don’t even always know how I got there. I’ll think I’m problem-solving, but really I’m just circling and circling.\n\nSo yes, it’s very hard to control. I can distract myself for a few minutes sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding when it stops.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired because of this worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that one. I do feel worn down a lot, but I don’t know if I can cleanly say, “Yes, it’s because of the worry,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… by the end of the day I can feel mentally spent from all the thinking and circling, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically tired more than other people would be. Sometimes I’ll sit down and realize I’ve been tense or preoccupied for hours, and that alone feels draining. But I don’t know that I’d say I’m always easily tired in a clear, obvious way.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as a separate thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes, I do. That one I can answer pretty clearly.

It happens a lot when I’m trying to fill out applications, or even when I’m trying to plan something simple, like a lesson idea. I’ll be reading a paragraph or a set of instructions, and then suddenly my mind has gone off to, “What is the administrator going to think of this?” or “Did I miss some important detail?” or “What if I answer this wrong and it looks careless?” And then I realize I’ve technically been looking at the words, but I haven’t absorbed them at all.

So I go back and read the same paragraph again… and then sometimes again. It’s embarrassing, because these aren’t complicated things, necessarily. I know how to read. I know how to plan a lesson. But my attention just keeps darting away from the actual task and toward what could go wrong or how I might be judged. It makes simple things take much longer than they should.

It just keeps finding another angle to worry from.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of worry been present more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s been more days than not, and honestly I’d say it’s been going on for about a year and two months now. It really started after I was fired from the preschool. Since then, it’s like the worrying just became part of the background of every day — not every single minute, but most days, yes. It didn’t just pass after a few weeks the way I hoped it would.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the worst parts of it, honestly. I can sometimes catch it starting, and I’ll try to be very practical about it — like, “Okay, make a list. What actually needs to be done today?” Or I’ll pray, or I’ll go outside and weed the garden because that usually gives my hands something simple to do. Or I’ll tell myself, “Just focus on the next step, not the whole future, just the next thing.”\n\nBut it doesn’t really stop. It just… changes shape. One worry hands off to the next one, like a relay race I didn’t agree to be in. I’ll start with one concern, and then by the time I look up, I’m three or four worries away from where I started, and half the afternoon is gone. And I don’t even always know how I got there. I’ll think I’m problem-solving, but really I’m just circling and circling.\n\nSo yes, it’s very hard to control. I can distract myself for a few minutes sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding when it stops.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired because of this worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that one. I do feel worn down a lot, but I don’t know if I can cleanly say, “Yes, it’s because of the worry,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… by the end of the day I can feel mentally spent from all the thinking and circling, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically tired more than other people would be. Sometimes I’ll sit down and realize I’ve been tense or preoccupied for hours, and that alone feels draining. But I don’t know that I’d say I’m always easily tired in a clear, obvious way.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as a separate thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate because of the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That one I can answer pretty clearly.\n\nIt happens a lot when I’m trying to fill out applications, or even when I’m trying to plan something simple, like a lesson idea. I’ll be reading a paragraph or a set of instructions, and then suddenly my mind has gone off to, “What is the administrator going to think of this?” or “Did I miss some important detail?” or “What if I answer this wrong and it looks careless?” And then I realize I’ve technically been looking at the words, but I haven’t absorbed them at all.\n\nSo I go back and read the same paragraph again… and then sometimes again. It’s embarrassing, because these aren’t complicated things, necessarily. I know how to read. I know how to plan a lesson. But my attention just keeps darting away from the actual task and toward what could go wrong or how I might be judged. It makes simple things take much longer than they should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mostly, yes, I think it started there, or at least that’s the thing my mind keeps coming back to. But it doesn’t stay neatly tied to just that one event anymore, if that makes sense.

It starts with being fired from the preschool — like, “How did this happen?” and “What are people saying?” and “Will another school ever take me seriously again?” But then it spreads. I start worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again at all, whether I’ve damaged my reputation with parents in a way I can’t repair, whether people will think there must have been something seriously wrong with me even if they don’t know the details.

And then it gets bigger than the job. I’ll think, “If I don’t get work again, what happens to retirement?” I’m 53, so it’s not like I’m just starting out and have endless time to rebuild everything. I start doing that kind of mental math over and over, and none of it feels settled.

And then, honestly, it even jumps to small things that don’t deserve that much weight. Like I’ll remember some tiny mistake from a story-time activity — maybe I stumbled over something or didn’t handle a moment perfectly — and my brain turns that into, “Maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like my mind is trying to prove I’m unsafe or incompetent somehow.

So yes, it’s connected to being fired, but it’s not just that one thing sitting in one box. It’s like everything related to my work, my reputation, my future, and whether I’m still the kind of teacher I thought I was — all of that gets pulled into it.

It didn’t just pass after a few weeks the way I hoped it would.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the worst parts of it, honestly. I can sometimes catch it starting, and I’ll try to be very practical about it — like, “Okay, make a list. What actually needs to be done today?” Or I’ll pray, or I’ll go outside and weed the garden because that usually gives my hands something simple to do. Or I’ll tell myself, “Just focus on the next step, not the whole future, just the next thing.”\n\nBut it doesn’t really stop. It just… changes shape. One worry hands off to the next one, like a relay race I didn’t agree to be in. I’ll start with one concern, and then by the time I look up, I’m three or four worries away from where I started, and half the afternoon is gone. And I don’t even always know how I got there. I’ll think I’m problem-solving, but really I’m just circling and circling.\n\nSo yes, it’s very hard to control. I can distract myself for a few minutes sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding when it stops.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired because of this worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that one. I do feel worn down a lot, but I don’t know if I can cleanly say, “Yes, it’s because of the worry,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… by the end of the day I can feel mentally spent from all the thinking and circling, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically tired more than other people would be. Sometimes I’ll sit down and realize I’ve been tense or preoccupied for hours, and that alone feels draining. But I don’t know that I’d say I’m always easily tired in a clear, obvious way.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as a separate thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate because of the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That one I can answer pretty clearly.\n\nIt happens a lot when I’m trying to fill out applications, or even when I’m trying to plan something simple, like a lesson idea. I’ll be reading a paragraph or a set of instructions, and then suddenly my mind has gone off to, “What is the administrator going to think of this?” or “Did I miss some important detail?” or “What if I answer this wrong and it looks careless?” And then I realize I’ve technically been looking at the words, but I haven’t absorbed them at all.\n\nSo I go back and read the same paragraph again… and then sometimes again. It’s embarrassing, because these aren’t complicated things, necessarily. I know how to read. I know how to plan a lesson. But my attention just keeps darting away from the actual task and toward what could go wrong or how I might be judged. It makes simple things take much longer than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that most of your anxiety is still mainly tied to being fired from the preschool?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, I think it started there, or at least that’s the thing my mind keeps coming back to. But it doesn’t stay neatly tied to just that one event anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nIt starts with being fired from the preschool — like, “How did this happen?” and “What are people saying?” and “Will another school ever take me seriously again?” But then it spreads. I start worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again at all, whether I’ve damaged my reputation with parents in a way I can’t repair, whether people will think there must have been something seriously wrong with me even if they don’t know the details.\n\nAnd then it gets bigger than the job. I’ll think, “If I don’t get work again, what happens to retirement?” I’m 53, so it’s not like I’m just starting out and have endless time to rebuild everything. I start doing that kind of mental math over and over, and none of it feels settled.\n\nAnd then, honestly, it even jumps to small things that don’t deserve that much weight. Like I’ll remember some tiny mistake from a story-time activity — maybe I stumbled over something or didn’t handle a moment perfectly — and my brain turns that into, “Maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like my mind is trying to prove I’m unsafe or incompetent somehow.\n\nSo yes, it’s connected to being fired, but it’s not just that one thing sitting in one box. It’s like everything related to my work, my reputation, my future, and whether I’m still the kind of teacher I thought I was — all of that gets pulled into it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as an irritable person, generally, so it’s a little hard to judge.

I haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people all the time or anything like that. But I suppose there are moments where I feel more… on edge, maybe, and small things can bother me more than I’d like. I don’t know if that counts as irritability, though, or if it’s just me being preoccupied and not having as much patience inside my own head.

Like, if someone asks me a question when I’m already caught up thinking about something, I might feel this little flash of annoyance—not necessarily because they did anything wrong, but because it interrupts the loop I’m in. But I don’t know that other people would say I’ve been noticeably more irritable. No one has really told me that, at least.

So I guess I’d say… maybe a little, but I’m not certain. It’s not the clearest thing I’ve noticed.

It’s like my body is bracing for a classroom observation that never ends, if that makes sense. I teach preschool and kindergarten, so I’m used to being “on,” but this feels different — like I can’t let my muscles stand down, even when nothing is actually happening.\n\nBy the time I get home in the evening, my jaw is aching from being tight all day. And I play guitar to unwind, or at least I used to, but lately I can hardly enjoy it because my jaw and neck are so sore and rigid. It’s embarrassing, maybe, that muscle tension is what got me here, but it’s constant enough now that I thought… okay, I need to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not just one worry that I can point to and say, “That’s the problem.” It kind of spreads everywhere, and my mind just keeps circling things over and over.\n\nLike, I’ll start thinking, what if I never get hired again if something goes wrong, or what if parents already think I’m not good at what I do? Then it becomes, have I ruined my reputation with families somehow? And then it jumps to bigger things, like whether retirement is slipping further away and I’m not going to be okay later on.\n\nAnd then it can get really… I don’t know, irrational-feeling but still convincing in the moment. I’ll think about some tiny mistake during a story-time activity — like maybe I handled something awkwardly or didn’t explain something clearly — and suddenly my brain is saying, “Does that mean you shouldn’t be trusted with children?” Which is awful to even say out loud, because I care so much about the kids. But that’s where my mind goes. It just keeps finding another angle to worry from.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of worry been present more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s been more days than not, and honestly I’d say it’s been going on for about a year and two months now. It really started after I was fired from the preschool. Since then, it’s like the worrying just became part of the background of every day — not every single minute, but most days, yes. It didn’t just pass after a few weeks the way I hoped it would.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the worst parts of it, honestly. I can sometimes catch it starting, and I’ll try to be very practical about it — like, “Okay, make a list. What actually needs to be done today?” Or I’ll pray, or I’ll go outside and weed the garden because that usually gives my hands something simple to do. Or I’ll tell myself, “Just focus on the next step, not the whole future, just the next thing.”\n\nBut it doesn’t really stop. It just… changes shape. One worry hands off to the next one, like a relay race I didn’t agree to be in. I’ll start with one concern, and then by the time I look up, I’m three or four worries away from where I started, and half the afternoon is gone. And I don’t even always know how I got there. I’ll think I’m problem-solving, but really I’m just circling and circling.\n\nSo yes, it’s very hard to control. I can distract myself for a few minutes sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding when it stops.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired because of this worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that one. I do feel worn down a lot, but I don’t know if I can cleanly say, “Yes, it’s because of the worry,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… by the end of the day I can feel mentally spent from all the thinking and circling, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically tired more than other people would be. Sometimes I’ll sit down and realize I’ve been tense or preoccupied for hours, and that alone feels draining. But I don’t know that I’d say I’m always easily tired in a clear, obvious way.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as a separate thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate because of the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That one I can answer pretty clearly.\n\nIt happens a lot when I’m trying to fill out applications, or even when I’m trying to plan something simple, like a lesson idea. I’ll be reading a paragraph or a set of instructions, and then suddenly my mind has gone off to, “What is the administrator going to think of this?” or “Did I miss some important detail?” or “What if I answer this wrong and it looks careless?” And then I realize I’ve technically been looking at the words, but I haven’t absorbed them at all.\n\nSo I go back and read the same paragraph again… and then sometimes again. It’s embarrassing, because these aren’t complicated things, necessarily. I know how to read. I know how to plan a lesson. But my attention just keeps darting away from the actual task and toward what could go wrong or how I might be judged. It makes simple things take much longer than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that most of your anxiety is still mainly tied to being fired from the preschool?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, I think it started there, or at least that’s the thing my mind keeps coming back to. But it doesn’t stay neatly tied to just that one event anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nIt starts with being fired from the preschool — like, “How did this happen?” and “What are people saying?” and “Will another school ever take me seriously again?” But then it spreads. I start worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again at all, whether I’ve damaged my reputation with parents in a way I can’t repair, whether people will think there must have been something seriously wrong with me even if they don’t know the details.\n\nAnd then it gets bigger than the job. I’ll think, “If I don’t get work again, what happens to retirement?” I’m 53, so it’s not like I’m just starting out and have endless time to rebuild everything. I start doing that kind of mental math over and over, and none of it feels settled.\n\nAnd then, honestly, it even jumps to small things that don’t deserve that much weight. Like I’ll remember some tiny mistake from a story-time activity — maybe I stumbled over something or didn’t handle a moment perfectly — and my brain turns that into, “Maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like my mind is trying to prove I’m unsafe or incompetent somehow.\n\nSo yes, it’s connected to being fired, but it’s not just that one thing sitting in one box. It’s like everything related to my work, my reputation, my future, and whether I’m still the kind of teacher I thought I was — all of that gets pulled into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as an irritable person, generally, so it’s a little hard to judge.\n\nI haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people all the time or anything like that. But I suppose there are moments where I feel more… on edge, maybe, and small things can bother me more than I’d like. I don’t know if that counts as irritability, though, or if it’s just me being preoccupied and not having as much patience inside my own head.\n\nLike, if someone asks me a question when I’m already caught up thinking about something, I might feel this little flash of annoyance—not necessarily because they did anything wrong, but because it interrupts the loop I’m in. But I don’t know that other people would say I’ve been noticeably more irritable. No one has really told me that, at least.\n\nSo I guess I’d say… maybe a little, but I’m not certain. It’s not the clearest thing I’ve noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not completely sure how to answer that one. I mean, I know what you mean in a general sense, like feeling keyed up or unable to settle, but I don’t know if I would describe myself that way consistently.

There are times when I feel… tense, maybe, or like I can’t quite let my mind come down. But I don’t know if that’s the same as restlessness. I’m not pacing around the room constantly or anything like that. I can sit still, at least outwardly. Sometimes I might look like I’m just sitting there normally, but inside I’m not exactly calm.

But it doesn’t stay neatly tied to just that one event anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nIt starts with being fired from the preschool — like, “How did this happen?” and “What are people saying?” and “Will another school ever take me seriously again?” But then it spreads. I start worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again at all, whether I’ve damaged my reputation with parents in a way I can’t repair, whether people will think there must have been something seriously wrong with me even if they don’t know the details.\n\nAnd then it gets bigger than the job. I’ll think, “If I don’t get work again, what happens to retirement?” I’m 53, so it’s not like I’m just starting out and have endless time to rebuild everything. I start doing that kind of mental math over and over, and none of it feels settled.\n\nAnd then, honestly, it even jumps to small things that don’t deserve that much weight. Like I’ll remember some tiny mistake from a story-time activity — maybe I stumbled over something or didn’t handle a moment perfectly — and my brain turns that into, “Maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like my mind is trying to prove I’m unsafe or incompetent somehow.\n\nSo yes, it’s connected to being fired, but it’s not just that one thing sitting in one box. It’s like everything related to my work, my reputation, my future, and whether I’m still the kind of teacher I thought I was — all of that gets pulled into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as an irritable person, generally, so it’s a little hard to judge.\n\nI haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people all the time or anything like that. But I suppose there are moments where I feel more… on edge, maybe, and small things can bother me more than I’d like. I don’t know if that counts as irritability, though, or if it’s just me being preoccupied and not having as much patience inside my own head.\n\nLike, if someone asks me a question when I’m already caught up thinking about something, I might feel this little flash of annoyance—not necessarily because they did anything wrong, but because it interrupts the loop I’m in. But I don’t know that other people would say I’ve been noticeably more irritable. No one has really told me that, at least.\n\nSo I guess I’d say… maybe a little, but I’m not certain. It’s not the clearest thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up, like you can’t fully relax?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that one. I mean, I know what you mean in a general sense, like feeling keyed up or unable to settle, but I don’t know if I would describe myself that way consistently.\n\nThere are times when I feel… tense, maybe, or like I can’t quite let my mind come down. But I don’t know if that’s the same as restlessness. I’m not pacing around the room constantly or anything like that. I can sit still, at least outwardly. Sometimes I might look like I’m just sitting there normally, but inside I’m not exactly calm.\n\nFully relaxing is hard to judge, too, because I think I’ve gotten used to not feeling completely relaxed. It’s not always dramatic. It’s more like there’s this background hum, and I don’t always notice it until someone asks me directly, like you just did. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say, yes, I’m restless all the time, because I’m not sure that’s accurate.\n\nSo… maybe somewhat, but I’m honestly not certain. It’s not one of the things I’ve tracked clearly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I want to say yes, that if the firing were fully resolved, most of it would go away… but I’m not completely sure anymore. I think the firing is the thing it attaches to most strongly, but my mind has made it much bigger than just, “I lost that job.”

It circles and circles around whether I’ll ever get hired again, and then whether parents have this idea of me now that I can’t undo, like my reputation has been ruined in some permanent way. And then I start thinking about retirement, because I’m 53, and I don’t feel like I have endless time to recover financially or professionally if this follows me.

And then it can shrink down to something tiny but somehow still feel huge. I’ll remember one little moment from a story-time activity where I maybe didn’t handle something perfectly, and suddenly my brain is treating that like evidence that maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. Which sounds awful to say, because teaching young children has been such a big part of who I am.

So if everything were resolved — if I knew I could work again, if I knew my name wasn’t damaged, if I knew the future wasn’t closing in — then yes, I imagine a lot of the anxiety would ease. But I also worry that my mind would still find something else to question, because lately it doesn’t seem satisfied with one answer. It keeps looking for another reason I might not be okay.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.9, F43.9, F45.41, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what finally pushed me to come in is how physically tense I’ve been. I mean, my shoulders and neck are just… clenched all day. It’s like my body is bracing for a classroom observation that never ends, if that makes sense. I teach preschool and kindergarten, so I’m used to being “on,” but this feels different — like I can’t let my muscles stand down, even when nothing is actually happening.\n\nBy the time I get home in the evening, my jaw is aching from being tight all day. And I play guitar to unwind, or at least I used to, but lately I can hardly enjoy it because my jaw and neck are so sore and rigid. It’s embarrassing, maybe, that muscle tension is what got me here, but it’s constant enough now that I thought… okay, I need to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not just one worry that I can point to and say, “That’s the problem.” It kind of spreads everywhere, and my mind just keeps circling things over and over.\n\nLike, I’ll start thinking, what if I never get hired again if something goes wrong, or what if parents already think I’m not good at what I do? Then it becomes, have I ruined my reputation with families somehow? And then it jumps to bigger things, like whether retirement is slipping further away and I’m not going to be okay later on.\n\nAnd then it can get really… I don’t know, irrational-feeling but still convincing in the moment. I’ll think about some tiny mistake during a story-time activity — like maybe I handled something awkwardly or didn’t explain something clearly — and suddenly my brain is saying, “Does that mean you shouldn’t be trusted with children?” Which is awful to even say out loud, because I care so much about the kids. But that’s where my mind goes. It just keeps finding another angle to worry from.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of worry been present more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s been more days than not, and honestly I’d say it’s been going on for about a year and two months now. It really started after I was fired from the preschool. Since then, it’s like the worrying just became part of the background of every day — not every single minute, but most days, yes. It didn’t just pass after a few weeks the way I hoped it would.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the worst parts of it, honestly. I can sometimes catch it starting, and I’ll try to be very practical about it — like, “Okay, make a list. What actually needs to be done today?” Or I’ll pray, or I’ll go outside and weed the garden because that usually gives my hands something simple to do. Or I’ll tell myself, “Just focus on the next step, not the whole future, just the next thing.”\n\nBut it doesn’t really stop. It just… changes shape. One worry hands off to the next one, like a relay race I didn’t agree to be in. I’ll start with one concern, and then by the time I look up, I’m three or four worries away from where I started, and half the afternoon is gone. And I don’t even always know how I got there. I’ll think I’m problem-solving, but really I’m just circling and circling.\n\nSo yes, it’s very hard to control. I can distract myself for a few minutes sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding when it stops.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired because of this worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that one. I do feel worn down a lot, but I don’t know if I can cleanly say, “Yes, it’s because of the worry,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… by the end of the day I can feel mentally spent from all the thinking and circling, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically tired more than other people would be. Sometimes I’ll sit down and realize I’ve been tense or preoccupied for hours, and that alone feels draining. But I don’t know that I’d say I’m always easily tired in a clear, obvious way.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as a separate thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate because of the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That one I can answer pretty clearly.\n\nIt happens a lot when I’m trying to fill out applications, or even when I’m trying to plan something simple, like a lesson idea. I’ll be reading a paragraph or a set of instructions, and then suddenly my mind has gone off to, “What is the administrator going to think of this?” or “Did I miss some important detail?” or “What if I answer this wrong and it looks careless?” And then I realize I’ve technically been looking at the words, but I haven’t absorbed them at all.\n\nSo I go back and read the same paragraph again… and then sometimes again. It’s embarrassing, because these aren’t complicated things, necessarily. I know how to read. I know how to plan a lesson. But my attention just keeps darting away from the actual task and toward what could go wrong or how I might be judged. It makes simple things take much longer than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that most of your anxiety is still mainly tied to being fired from the preschool?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, I think it started there, or at least that’s the thing my mind keeps coming back to. But it doesn’t stay neatly tied to just that one event anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nIt starts with being fired from the preschool — like, “How did this happen?” and “What are people saying?” and “Will another school ever take me seriously again?” But then it spreads. I start worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again at all, whether I’ve damaged my reputation with parents in a way I can’t repair, whether people will think there must have been something seriously wrong with me even if they don’t know the details.\n\nAnd then it gets bigger than the job. I’ll think, “If I don’t get work again, what happens to retirement?” I’m 53, so it’s not like I’m just starting out and have endless time to rebuild everything. I start doing that kind of mental math over and over, and none of it feels settled.\n\nAnd then, honestly, it even jumps to small things that don’t deserve that much weight. Like I’ll remember some tiny mistake from a story-time activity — maybe I stumbled over something or didn’t handle a moment perfectly — and my brain turns that into, “Maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like my mind is trying to prove I’m unsafe or incompetent somehow.\n\nSo yes, it’s connected to being fired, but it’s not just that one thing sitting in one box. It’s like everything related to my work, my reputation, my future, and whether I’m still the kind of teacher I thought I was — all of that gets pulled into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as an irritable person, generally, so it’s a little hard to judge.\n\nI haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people all the time or anything like that. But I suppose there are moments where I feel more… on edge, maybe, and small things can bother me more than I’d like. I don’t know if that counts as irritability, though, or if it’s just me being preoccupied and not having as much patience inside my own head.\n\nLike, if someone asks me a question when I’m already caught up thinking about something, I might feel this little flash of annoyance—not necessarily because they did anything wrong, but because it interrupts the loop I’m in. But I don’t know that other people would say I’ve been noticeably more irritable. No one has really told me that, at least.\n\nSo I guess I’d say… maybe a little, but I’m not certain. It’s not the clearest thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up, like you can’t fully relax?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that one. I mean, I know what you mean in a general sense, like feeling keyed up or unable to settle, but I don’t know if I would describe myself that way consistently.\n\nThere are times when I feel… tense, maybe, or like I can’t quite let my mind come down. But I don’t know if that’s the same as restlessness. I’m not pacing around the room constantly or anything like that. I can sit still, at least outwardly. Sometimes I might look like I’m just sitting there normally, but inside I’m not exactly calm.\n\nFully relaxing is hard to judge, too, because I think I’ve gotten used to not feeling completely relaxed. It’s not always dramatic. It’s more like there’s this background hum, and I don’t always notice it until someone asks me directly, like you just did. It’s not one of the things I’ve tracked clearly.\n\nDoctor: If you imagine that the firing and its consequences were fully resolved, do you think most of this anxiety would still be there?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, that if the firing were fully resolved, most of it would go away… but I’m not completely sure anymore.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… definitely. Sleep has probably been one of the clearest ways this is showing up.

Most nights I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then my mind just starts going. I’ll replay things I said in interviews, or imagine how I should have answered differently, or whether I sounded defensive or incompetent. Then it shifts to bills, and what happens if money runs out, and then I start thinking about forms or licensing paperwork and whether I’ve missed something important. It’s like once it gets quiet, my brain treats that as an opportunity to bring up every possible problem.

I can be lying there for hours. Not necessarily moving around a lot, just staring in the dark, trying to convince myself to stop thinking, which of course doesn’t work. And then even when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel settled. I’ve been waking up around four in the morning a lot, already tense, like my body is starting the day before I’m even fully awake. I’ll immediately begin calculating what could go wrong that day, what I need to do, what I forgot, what might come in the mail, who might contact me.

So yes, I’m having trouble sleeping. It’s not just one bad night here and there. It’s most nights, and it makes the mornings feel like I’m already behind before the day has even started.

It’s like my body is bracing for a classroom observation that never ends, if that makes sense. I teach preschool and kindergarten, so I’m used to being “on,” but this feels different — like I can’t let my muscles stand down, even when nothing is actually happening.\n\nBy the time I get home in the evening, my jaw is aching from being tight all day. And I play guitar to unwind, or at least I used to, but lately I can hardly enjoy it because my jaw and neck are so sore and rigid. It’s embarrassing, maybe, that muscle tension is what got me here, but it’s constant enough now that I thought… okay, I need to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not just one worry that I can point to and say, “That’s the problem.” It kind of spreads everywhere, and my mind just keeps circling things over and over.\n\nLike, I’ll start thinking, what if I never get hired again if something goes wrong, or what if parents already think I’m not good at what I do? Then it becomes, have I ruined my reputation with families somehow? And then it jumps to bigger things, like whether retirement is slipping further away and I’m not going to be okay later on.\n\nAnd then it can get really… I don’t know, irrational-feeling but still convincing in the moment. I’ll think about some tiny mistake during a story-time activity — like maybe I handled something awkwardly or didn’t explain something clearly — and suddenly my brain is saying, “Does that mean you shouldn’t be trusted with children?” Which is awful to even say out loud, because I care so much about the kids. But that’s where my mind goes. It just keeps finding another angle to worry from.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of worry been present more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s been more days than not, and honestly I’d say it’s been going on for about a year and two months now. It really started after I was fired from the preschool. Since then, it’s like the worrying just became part of the background of every day — not every single minute, but most days, yes. It didn’t just pass after a few weeks the way I hoped it would.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the worst parts of it, honestly. I can sometimes catch it starting, and I’ll try to be very practical about it — like, “Okay, make a list. What actually needs to be done today?” Or I’ll pray, or I’ll go outside and weed the garden because that usually gives my hands something simple to do. Or I’ll tell myself, “Just focus on the next step, not the whole future, just the next thing.”\n\nBut it doesn’t really stop. It just… changes shape. One worry hands off to the next one, like a relay race I didn’t agree to be in. I’ll start with one concern, and then by the time I look up, I’m three or four worries away from where I started, and half the afternoon is gone. And I don’t even always know how I got there. I’ll think I’m problem-solving, but really I’m just circling and circling.\n\nSo yes, it’s very hard to control. I can distract myself for a few minutes sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding when it stops.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired because of this worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that one. I do feel worn down a lot, but I don’t know if I can cleanly say, “Yes, it’s because of the worry,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… by the end of the day I can feel mentally spent from all the thinking and circling, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically tired more than other people would be. Sometimes I’ll sit down and realize I’ve been tense or preoccupied for hours, and that alone feels draining. But I don’t know that I’d say I’m always easily tired in a clear, obvious way.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as a separate thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate because of the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That one I can answer pretty clearly.\n\nIt happens a lot when I’m trying to fill out applications, or even when I’m trying to plan something simple, like a lesson idea. I’ll be reading a paragraph or a set of instructions, and then suddenly my mind has gone off to, “What is the administrator going to think of this?” or “Did I miss some important detail?” or “What if I answer this wrong and it looks careless?” And then I realize I’ve technically been looking at the words, but I haven’t absorbed them at all.\n\nSo I go back and read the same paragraph again… and then sometimes again. It’s embarrassing, because these aren’t complicated things, necessarily. I know how to read. I know how to plan a lesson. But my attention just keeps darting away from the actual task and toward what could go wrong or how I might be judged. It makes simple things take much longer than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that most of your anxiety is still mainly tied to being fired from the preschool?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, I think it started there, or at least that’s the thing my mind keeps coming back to. But it doesn’t stay neatly tied to just that one event anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nIt starts with being fired from the preschool — like, “How did this happen?” and “What are people saying?” and “Will another school ever take me seriously again?” But then it spreads. I start worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again at all, whether I’ve damaged my reputation with parents in a way I can’t repair, whether people will think there must have been something seriously wrong with me even if they don’t know the details.\n\nAnd then it gets bigger than the job. I’ll think, “If I don’t get work again, what happens to retirement?” I’m 53, so it’s not like I’m just starting out and have endless time to rebuild everything. I start doing that kind of mental math over and over, and none of it feels settled.\n\nAnd then, honestly, it even jumps to small things that don’t deserve that much weight. Like I’ll remember some tiny mistake from a story-time activity — maybe I stumbled over something or didn’t handle a moment perfectly — and my brain turns that into, “Maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like my mind is trying to prove I’m unsafe or incompetent somehow.\n\nSo yes, it’s connected to being fired, but it’s not just that one thing sitting in one box. It’s like everything related to my work, my reputation, my future, and whether I’m still the kind of teacher I thought I was — all of that gets pulled into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. It’s not the clearest thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up, like you can’t fully relax?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that one. Like I’ll remember some tiny mistake from a story-time activity — maybe I stumbled over something or didn’t handle a moment perfectly — and my brain turns that into, “Maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like my mind is trying to prove I’m unsafe or incompetent somehow.\n\nSo yes, it’s connected to being fired, but it’s not just that one thing sitting in one box. It’s like everything related to my work, my reputation, my future, and whether I’m still the kind of teacher I thought I was — all of that gets pulled into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as an irritable person, generally, so it’s a little hard to judge.\n\nI haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people all the time or anything like that. But I suppose there are moments where I feel more… on edge, maybe, and small things can bother me more than I’d like. I don’t know if that counts as irritability, though, or if it’s just me being preoccupied and not having as much patience inside my own head.\n\nLike, if someone asks me a question when I’m already caught up thinking about something, I might feel this little flash of annoyance—not necessarily because they did anything wrong, but because it interrupts the loop I’m in. But I don’t know that other people would say I’ve been noticeably more irritable. No one has really told me that, at least.\n\nSo I guess I’d say… maybe a little, but I’m not certain. It’s not the clearest thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up, like you can’t fully relax?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that one. I mean, I know what you mean in a general sense, like feeling keyed up or unable to settle, but I don’t know if I would describe myself that way consistently.\n\nThere are times when I feel… tense, maybe, or like I can’t quite let my mind come down. But I don’t know if that’s the same as restlessness. I’m not pacing around the room constantly or anything like that. I can sit still, at least outwardly. Sometimes I might look like I’m just sitting there normally, but inside I’m not exactly calm.\n\nFully relaxing is hard to judge, too, because I think I’ve gotten used to not feeling completely relaxed. It’s not always dramatic. It’s more like there’s this background hum, and I don’t always notice it until someone asks me directly, like you just did. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say, yes, I’m restless all the time, because I’m not sure that’s accurate.\n\nSo… maybe somewhat, but I’m honestly not certain. It’s not one of the things I’ve tracked clearly.\n\nDoctor: If you imagine that the firing and its consequences were fully resolved, do you think most of this anxiety would still be there?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, that if the firing were fully resolved, most of it would go away… but I’m not completely sure anymore. I think the firing is the thing it attaches to most strongly, but my mind has made it much bigger than just, “I lost that job.”\n\nIt circles and circles around whether I’ll ever get hired again, and then whether parents have this idea of me now that I can’t undo, like my reputation has been ruined in some permanent way. And then I start thinking about retirement, because I’m 53, and I don’t feel like I have endless time to recover financially or professionally if this follows me.\n\nAnd then it can shrink down to something tiny but somehow still feel huge. I’ll remember one little moment from a story-time activity where I maybe didn’t handle something perfectly, and suddenly my brain is treating that like evidence that maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. Which sounds awful to say, because teaching young children has been such a big part of who I am.\n\nSo if everything were resolved — if I knew I could work again, if I knew my name wasn’t damaged, if I knew the future wasn’t closing in — then yes, I imagine a lot of the anxiety would ease. But I also worry that my mind would still find something else to question, because lately it doesn’t seem satisfied with one answer. It keeps looking for another reason I might not be okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. Sleep has probably been one of the clearest ways this is showing up.\n\nMost nights I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then my mind just starts going. I’ll replay things I said in interviews, or imagine how I should have answered differently, or whether I sounded defensive or incompetent. Then it shifts to bills, and what happens if money runs out, and then I start thinking about forms or licensing paperwork and whether I’ve missed something important. It’s like once it gets quiet, my brain treats that as an opportunity to bring up every possible problem.\n\nI can be lying there for hours. Not necessarily moving around a lot, just staring in the dark, trying to convince myself to stop thinking, which of course doesn’t work. And then even when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel settled. I’ve been waking up around four in the morning a lot, already tense, like my body is starting the day before I’m even fully awake. I’ll immediately begin calculating what could go wrong that day, what I need to do, what I forgot, what might come in the mail, who might contact me.\n\nSo yes, I’m having trouble sleeping. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it probably is stronger than what most people would feel, even though part of me hesitates to say that, because losing a job is a big thing. I don’t want to act like anyone would just shrug that off.

But with me, it feels like my mind doesn’t stop at the normal worries. It doesn’t just stay with, “I need to find another position.” It keeps circling from whether I’ll ever get hired again, to whether I’ve somehow ruined my reputation with parents, to whether retirement is slipping away because I’m 53 and maybe I don’t have enough time to recover from this.

And then it goes even further, which is the part that makes me think maybe it’s out of proportion. I’ll start thinking about some tiny moment from a story-time activity, like maybe I handled one small thing imperfectly, and suddenly my mind is treating that as if it means something terrible about me — like maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. And when I say that out loud, I know it sounds extreme, because I’ve spent my career working with young kids. But in the moment, my brain makes it feel like real evidence.

So yes, I think most people would be upset and worried after losing a job. But I don’t know if most people would have their thoughts spread out like this, into every corner of their life and identity. That’s what makes me feel like mine is stronger than it should be.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.9, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what finally pushed me to come in is how physically tense I’ve been. I mean, my shoulders and neck are just… clenched all day. It’s like my body is bracing for a classroom observation that never ends, if that makes sense. I teach preschool and kindergarten, so I’m used to being “on,” but this feels different — like I can’t let my muscles stand down, even when nothing is actually happening.\n\nBy the time I get home in the evening, my jaw is aching from being tight all day. And I play guitar to unwind, or at least I used to, but lately I can hardly enjoy it because my jaw and neck are so sore and rigid. It’s embarrassing, maybe, that muscle tension is what got me here, but it’s constant enough now that I thought… okay, I need to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not just one worry that I can point to and say, “That’s the problem.” It kind of spreads everywhere, and my mind just keeps circling things over and over.\n\nLike, I’ll start thinking, what if I never get hired again if something goes wrong, or what if parents already think I’m not good at what I do? Then it becomes, have I ruined my reputation with families somehow? And then it jumps to bigger things, like whether retirement is slipping further away and I’m not going to be okay later on.\n\nAnd then it can get really… I don’t know, irrational-feeling but still convincing in the moment. I’ll think about some tiny mistake during a story-time activity — like maybe I handled something awkwardly or didn’t explain something clearly — and suddenly my brain is saying, “Does that mean you shouldn’t be trusted with children?” Which is awful to even say out loud, because I care so much about the kids. But that’s where my mind goes. It just keeps finding another angle to worry from.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of worry been present more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s been more days than not, and honestly I’d say it’s been going on for about a year and two months now. It really started after I was fired from the preschool. Since then, it’s like the worrying just became part of the background of every day — not every single minute, but most days, yes. It didn’t just pass after a few weeks the way I hoped it would.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the worst parts of it, honestly. I can sometimes catch it starting, and I’ll try to be very practical about it — like, “Okay, make a list. What actually needs to be done today?” Or I’ll pray, or I’ll go outside and weed the garden because that usually gives my hands something simple to do. Or I’ll tell myself, “Just focus on the next step, not the whole future, just the next thing.”\n\nBut it doesn’t really stop. It just… changes shape. One worry hands off to the next one, like a relay race I didn’t agree to be in. I’ll start with one concern, and then by the time I look up, I’m three or four worries away from where I started, and half the afternoon is gone. And I don’t even always know how I got there. I’ll think I’m problem-solving, but really I’m just circling and circling.\n\nSo yes, it’s very hard to control. I can distract myself for a few minutes sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding when it stops.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired because of this worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that one. I do feel worn down a lot, but I don’t know if I can cleanly say, “Yes, it’s because of the worry,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… by the end of the day I can feel mentally spent from all the thinking and circling, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically tired more than other people would be. Sometimes I’ll sit down and realize I’ve been tense or preoccupied for hours, and that alone feels draining. But I don’t know that I’d say I’m always easily tired in a clear, obvious way.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as a separate thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate because of the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That one I can answer pretty clearly.\n\nIt happens a lot when I’m trying to fill out applications, or even when I’m trying to plan something simple, like a lesson idea. I’ll be reading a paragraph or a set of instructions, and then suddenly my mind has gone off to, “What is the administrator going to think of this?” or “Did I miss some important detail?” or “What if I answer this wrong and it looks careless?” And then I realize I’ve technically been looking at the words, but I haven’t absorbed them at all.\n\nSo I go back and read the same paragraph again… and then sometimes again. It’s embarrassing, because these aren’t complicated things, necessarily. I know how to read. I know how to plan a lesson. But my attention just keeps darting away from the actual task and toward what could go wrong or how I might be judged. It makes simple things take much longer than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that most of your anxiety is still mainly tied to being fired from the preschool?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, I think it started there, or at least that’s the thing my mind keeps coming back to. But it doesn’t stay neatly tied to just that one event anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nIt starts with being fired from the preschool — like, “How did this happen?” and “What are people saying?” and “Will another school ever take me seriously again?” But then it spreads. I start worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again at all, whether I’ve damaged my reputation with parents in a way I can’t repair, whether people will think there must have been something seriously wrong with me even if they don’t know the details.\n\nAnd then it gets bigger than the job. I’ll think, “If I don’t get work again, what happens to retirement?” I’m 53, so it’s not like I’m just starting out and have endless time to rebuild everything. I start doing that kind of mental math over and over, and none of it feels settled.\n\nAnd then, honestly, it even jumps to small things that don’t deserve that much weight. Like I’ll remember some tiny mistake from a story-time activity — maybe I stumbled over something or didn’t handle a moment perfectly — and my brain turns that into, “Maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like my mind is trying to prove I’m unsafe or incompetent somehow.\n\nSo yes, it’s connected to being fired, but it’s not just that one thing sitting in one box. It’s like everything related to my work, my reputation, my future, and whether I’m still the kind of teacher I thought I was — all of that gets pulled into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as an irritable person, generally, so it’s a little hard to judge.\n\nI haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people all the time or anything like that. But I suppose there are moments where I feel more… on edge, maybe, and small things can bother me more than I’d like. I don’t know if that counts as irritability, though, or if it’s just me being preoccupied and not having as much patience inside my own head.\n\nLike, if someone asks me a question when I’m already caught up thinking about something, I might feel this little flash of annoyance—not necessarily because they did anything wrong, but because it interrupts the loop I’m in. But I don’t know that other people would say I’ve been noticeably more irritable. No one has really told me that, at least.\n\nSo I guess I’d say… maybe a little, but I’m not certain. It’s not the clearest thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up, like you can’t fully relax?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that one. I mean, I know what you mean in a general sense, like feeling keyed up or unable to settle, but I don’t know if I would describe myself that way consistently.\n\nThere are times when I feel… tense, maybe, or like I can’t quite let my mind come down. But I don’t know if that’s the same as restlessness. I’m not pacing around the room constantly or anything like that. I can sit still, at least outwardly. Sometimes I might look like I’m just sitting there normally, but inside I’m not exactly calm.\n\nFully relaxing is hard to judge, too, because I think I’ve gotten used to not feeling completely relaxed. It’s not always dramatic. It’s more like there’s this background hum, and I don’t always notice it until someone asks me directly, like you just did. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say, yes, I’m restless all the time, because I’m not sure that’s accurate.\n\nSo… maybe somewhat, but I’m honestly not certain. It’s not one of the things I’ve tracked clearly.\n\nDoctor: If you imagine that the firing and its consequences were fully resolved, do you think most of this anxiety would still be there?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, that if the firing were fully resolved, most of it would go away… but I’m not completely sure anymore. I think the firing is the thing it attaches to most strongly, but my mind has made it much bigger than just, “I lost that job.”\n\nIt circles and circles around whether I’ll ever get hired again, and then whether parents have this idea of me now that I can’t undo, like my reputation has been ruined in some permanent way. And then I start thinking about retirement, because I’m 53, and I don’t feel like I have endless time to recover financially or professionally if this follows me.\n\nAnd then it can shrink down to something tiny but somehow still feel huge. I’ll remember one little moment from a story-time activity where I maybe didn’t handle something perfectly, and suddenly my brain is treating that like evidence that maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. Which sounds awful to say, because teaching young children has been such a big part of who I am.\n\nSo if everything were resolved — if I knew I could work again, if I knew my name wasn’t damaged, if I knew the future wasn’t closing in — then yes, I imagine a lot of the anxiety would ease. But I also worry that my mind would still find something else to question, because lately it doesn’t seem satisfied with one answer. It keeps looking for another reason I might not be okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. Sleep has probably been one of the clearest ways this is showing up.\n\nMost nights I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then my mind just starts going. I’ll replay things I said in interviews, or imagine how I should have answered differently, or whether I sounded defensive or incompetent. Then it shifts to bills, and what happens if money runs out, and then I start thinking about forms or licensing paperwork and whether I’ve missed something important. It’s like once it gets quiet, my brain treats that as an opportunity to bring up every possible problem.\n\nI can be lying there for hours. Not necessarily moving around a lot, just staring in the dark, trying to convince myself to stop thinking, which of course doesn’t work. And then even when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel settled. I’ve been waking up around four in the morning a lot, already tense, like my body is starting the day before I’m even fully awake. I’ll immediately begin calculating what could go wrong that day, what I need to do, what I forgot, what might come in the mail, who might contact me.\n\nSo yes, I’m having trouble sleeping. It’s not just one bad night here and there. It’s most nights, and it makes the mornings feel like I’m already behind before the day has even started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is much stronger than what most people would experience after losing a job?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is stronger than what most people would feel, even though part of me hesitates to say that, because losing a job is a big thing. I don’t want to act like anyone would just shrug that off.\n\nBut with me, it feels like my mind doesn’t stop at the normal worries. It doesn’t just stay with, “I need to find another position.” It keeps circling from whether I’ll ever get hired again, to whether I’ve somehow ruined my reputation with parents, to whether retirement is slipping away because I’m 53 and maybe I don’t have enough time to recover from this.\n\nAnd then it goes even further, which is the part that makes me think maybe it’s out of proportion. I’ll start thinking about some tiny moment from a story-time activity, like maybe I handled one small thing imperfectly, and suddenly my mind is treating that as if it means something terrible about me — like maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. And when I say that out loud, I know it sounds extreme, because I’ve spent my career working with young kids. But in the moment, my brain makes it feel like real evidence.\n\nSo yes, I think most people would be upset and worried after losing a job. But I don’t know if most people would have their thoughts spread out like this, into every corner of their life and identity. That’s what makes me feel like mine is stronger than it should be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Generalized Anxiety Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: If you imagine that the firing and its consequences were fully resolved, do you think most of this anxiety would still be there?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, that if the firing were fully resolved, most of it would go away… but I’m not completely sure anymore. I think the firing is the thing it attaches to most strongly, but my mind has made it much bigger than just, “I lost that job.”\n\nIt circles and circles around whether I’ll ever get hired again, and then whether parents have this idea of me now that I can’t undo, like my reputation has been ruined in some permanent way. And then I start thinking about retirement, because I’m 53, and I don’t feel like I have endless time to recover financially or professionally if this follows me.\n\nAnd then it can shrink down to something tiny but somehow still feel huge. I’ll remember one little moment from a story-time activity where I maybe didn’t handle something perfectly, and suddenly my brain is treating that like evidence that maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. Which sounds awful to say, because teaching young children has been such a big part of who I am.\n\nSo if everything were resolved — if I knew I could work again, if I knew my name wasn’t damaged, if I knew the future wasn’t closing in — then yes, I imagine a lot of the anxiety would ease. But I also worry that my mind would still find something else to question, because lately it doesn’t seem satisfied with one answer. It keeps looking for another reason I might not be okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. Sleep has probably been one of the clearest ways this is showing up.\n\nMost nights I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then my mind just starts going. I’ll replay things I said in interviews, or imagine how I should have answered differently, or whether I sounded defensive or incompetent. Then it shifts to bills, and what happens if money runs out, and then I start thinking about forms or licensing paperwork and whether I’ve missed something important. It’s like once it gets quiet, my brain treats that as an opportunity to bring up every possible problem.\n\nI can be lying there for hours. Not necessarily moving around a lot, just staring in the dark, trying to convince myself to stop thinking, which of course doesn’t work. And then even when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel settled. I’ve been waking up around four in the morning a lot, already tense, like my body is starting the day before I’m even fully awake. I’ll immediately begin calculating what could go wrong that day, what I need to do, what I forgot, what might come in the mail, who might contact me.\n\nSo yes, I’m having trouble sleeping. It’s not just one bad night here and there. It’s most nights, and it makes the mornings feel like I’m already behind before the day has even started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is much stronger than what most people would experience after losing a job?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is stronger than what most people would feel, even though part of me hesitates to say that, because losing a job is a big thing. I don’t want to act like anyone would just shrug that off.\n\nBut with me, it feels like my mind doesn’t stop at the normal worries. It doesn’t just stay with, “I need to find another position.” It keeps circling from whether I’ll ever get hired again, to whether I’ve somehow ruined my reputation with parents, to whether retirement is slipping away because I’m 53 and maybe I don’t have enough time to recover from this.\n\nAnd then it goes even further, which is the part that makes me think maybe it’s out of proportion. I’ll start thinking about some tiny moment from a story-time activity, like maybe I handled one small thing imperfectly, and suddenly my mind is treating that as if it means something terrible about me — like maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. And when I say that out loud, I know it sounds extreme, because I’ve spent my career working with young kids. But in the moment, my brain makes it feel like real evidence.\n\nSo yes, I think most people would be upset and worried after losing a job. But I don’t know if most people would have their thoughts spread out like this, into every corner of their life and identity. That’s what makes me feel like mine is stronger than it should be.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore you were fired from the preschool, did you already tend to worry this much about many different areas of your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 53 / preschool_or_kindergarten_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about a year and two months, since I was fired from the preschool.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not like this. I mean, I’ve always been someone who thinks things through, maybe too much sometimes, but this level of worry really started after I was fired from the preschool.

It’s been about a year and two months now, and since then it feels like the worry has just stayed with me. Before that, I wouldn’t say I was carefree, exactly, but I don’t remember my mind grabbing onto so many different things with this kind of intensity. The firing was the point where it all seemed to begin, or at least where it became something I couldn’t just set aside.

So, no, I don’t think I was worrying this much across my life before then. It’s really been since that happened — for about fourteen months.
